You are a neutral, skeptical reviewer for a research paper. Working directory: /root/paper/foyer

Read these files (do not modify anything):
- paper/main.tex, paper/sec_intro.tex, paper/sec_model.tex, paper/sec_criterion.tex, paper/sec_results.tex, paper/sec_discussion.tex
- paper/data/*.csv  (especially reuse.csv, eviction.csv, concurrency.csv, runs.csv, decomp.csv, elasticity.csv, elasticity_hc.csv, replicates.csv)
- scripts/lab/gen_reuse.py, scripts/lab/gen_concurrency.py, scripts/lab/frontier.py, scripts/lab/gen_table.py
- docs/finding_concurrency_contamination_20260924.md, docs/finding_hicache_elasticity_20260923.md
- CLAUDE.md

CONTEXT: This is a NEGATIVE-RESULT measurement paper on admission control for memory-bound agentic LLM serving
(200 sessions, one RTX 3090, Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, SGLang, 101,432-token KV pool). Nine controller variants, none
beats the static Pareto front. Target venue: CCF-B journal / CAS Q2. The authors just RESTRUCTURED the paper
around a new main claim.

FOCUS THIS ROUND on three things:

(1) DOES THE NEW MAIN CLAIM HOLD? The paper now claims: "prefix reuse has a ceiling set by the workload
(19.9M of 28.5M prompt tokens); whether that ceiling is realized is set by the memory tier."
 - Is the two-way split (ceiling = workload property, realization = config property) supported by the data,
   or is it circular / unfalsifiable as presented?
 - The key contrast is cap3_fixed (device-only, occupancy 55.8%, realizes 59.6%) vs cap3_hc (host tier,
   occupancy 55.5%, realizes 68.8%). Are these two runs comparable? They ran on different dates. Name any
   confound the paper fails to rule out.
 - The paper states a PREDICTION (host tier multiplies effective capacity ~3x, so the reuse collapse should
   move to ~3x the running count). Experiments testing it (cap5_hc/cap6_hc/cap8_hc) are RUNNING and have NO
   RESULTS YET. Does the text anywhere use the prediction as if it were established? Quote the exact sentence.

(2) IS THE CO-TENANCY CONTAMINATION HANDLED HONESTLY? New finding: 12 runs executed with >=4 concurrent
engine instances on one host; their client-measured per-request decode interval is inflated 25-40%. Four of
them (foyer_bres, foyer_hz0, foyer_hz2, foyer_breshz1) are in Table I.
 - Check §repro's account against paper/data/concurrency.csv. Are the counts right?
 - The text says those four "have been re-measured". Check whether that is TRUE right now (look at
   results/night/ for *_r2 directories and whether they contain summary.json). If the re-runs have not
   finished, is the claim premature?
 - The paper argues the contaminated runs' VOLUME measurements (reuse, recomputation) are unaffected.
   Is that argument sound, or could contention also change cache behaviour? What evidence would settle it?

(3) DID THE RESTRUCTURE BREAK ANYTHING?
 - Verify the new numbers are traceable: 69.8 / 27.4 / 59.6 / 68.8 / 68.1 / 19.9 / 28.5 / 8.6 / 20.7.
 - Two different normalizations are in play: the new reuse fraction is ALL-STEPS TOKEN-WEIGHTED, while
   Drelated Work quotes a ROUND-1+ UNWEIGHTED MEAN hit rate (80.8->32.8 device-only, 80.7->78.7 host tier).
   Does any sentence mix them?
 - Is any RETRACTED claim (see CLAUDE.md retraction list and paper/claims_audit.md) revived by the new text?
 - Any claim that is stronger than its evidence?

OUTPUT FORMAT (be specific, cite file:line):
## Major issues
## Minor issues
## Questions requiring clarification

Then, separately:
## Acceptance assessment
Give a rough probability range that this would be accepted at a CCF-B journal / CAS Q2 venue AS IT STANDS,
with the reasoning. This is a judgement, not a prediction -- say what it rests on. Be calibrated, not kind.
Do NOT invent review scores or impersonate a specific venue's reviewer.

    29	\title{When Does Admission Control Pay?\\
    30	The Memory Tier Sets the Cost of Concurrency in Agentic LLM Serving}
    31	
    32	\author{\IEEEauthorblockN{Anonymous Submission}}
    33	
    34	\maketitle
    35	
    36	\begin{abstract}
    37	Agentic LLM sessions are long, multi-round jobs whose KV footprint grows every
    38	round, and on a memory-constrained accelerator the pool is the binding
    39	resource. Admission control therefore looks like the obvious lever: a
    40	controller that reads engine telemetry and admits on measured capacity should
    41	beat a static cap. We give a measurement that says how much
    42	room there is, and show what happens when you build against it. Throughput during busy time is
    43	$\overline{R}/\tau$---running requests over per-step cost---so raising
    44	concurrency by a factor $f$ buys $f^{1-\epsilon}$, where
    45	$\epsilon = \mathrm{d}\ln\tau / \mathrm{d}\ln\overline{R}$. Near zero, busy-time throughput keeps
    46	converting; near one it does not. It is a property of the workload \emph{and}
    47	the engine configuration, not of the controller, and what sets it is the
    48	memory tier. Prefix reuse has a ceiling the workload fixes---19.9\,M of our
    49	28.5\,M prompt tokens---and the tier decides whether it is realized:
    50	device-only, reuse collapses from 69.8\% to 27.4\% as the running count rises
    51	from 1.00 to 1.84 and the shortfall returns one for one as recomputation
    52	(8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens); with a host tier the ceiling holds, 69.8\% to
    53	68.1\% between 1.25 and 1.73, while the \emph{device} cache misses more. Two
    54	configurations at the same running count and the same pool occupancy realize
    55	59.6\% and 68.8\% of it, so it is not how full the pool is but what happens
    56	when it overflows. Our controllers run on the host-tier configuration, where
    57	$\epsilon$ stays below one across their whole operating range, so the ceiling
    58	we measure on device-only caps is not what bounded them. It bounds what added concurrency is worth, not what a
    59	whole run achieves---idle time and the SLO axis are separate. We measure $\epsilon$ on a
    60	memory-bound agentic replay (200 sessions, one 24\,GB GPU, a pool that holds
    61	four median sessions) and get $0.38$ to $0.61$ depending on which step cost a
    62	system can observe: every 10\% of added concurrency buys 4--6\% of throughput.
    63	
    64	The verdict that criterion returns here is negative, and we report it at that
    65	strength. Nine controller variants spanning four routes, plus a
    66	reimplementation of an independent congestion-based controller, none is
    67	above the static Pareto front at the latency threshold interactive serving
    68	cares about---the best misses it by under a point, on the below side of our
    69	tie band, and its repeat misses by 5.6---; a plain cap on resident sessions
    70	remains the fastest policy we measured. That is not an argument for admitting everything: with no control at
    71	all the median request waits 1{,}774 seconds for its first token, the prefix
    72	cache collapses from a 75\% planned hit rate to 1.6\%, and SLO falls to 3\%.
    73	The question is which selective rule, not whether to be selective. Two
    74	measurable effects, rather than one we can tune away, account for part of why
    75	our variants fail---the arm-by-arm attribution of
    76	\S\ref{sec:results:attribution} covers four of the eleven arms we tested and
    77	leaves seven as residuals we can measure and cannot yet explain. They hold
    78	nearly half
    79	the pool idle while sessions queue---their own headroom estimate on the base
    80	configuration is 1.79$\times$ the truth (1.0--4.1$\times$ across all of them)---and the per-step elasticity measured on the
    81	configuration they actually run stays below one across their whole operating

     1	\section{Introduction}
     2	\label{sec:intro}
     3	
     4	An agentic LLM session is not a request. It is a long-lived job that issues a
     5	round of inference, waits on a tool, and comes back---tens of times---with a
     6	context that grows every round. On a memory-constrained accelerator this makes
     7	admission control look like the obvious lever. The KV pool is the binding
     8	resource; a single large session can occupy most of it; and the sessions
     9	themselves are wildly heterogeneous. If a scheduler can see how much of the
    10	pool a candidate will need and how much room is left, surely it should beat a
    11	fixed number.
    12	
    13	We believed this, built a controller that does exactly that, and spent a
    14	considerable amount of GPU time discovering that on our workload and hardware
    15	it is false. This paper reports that negative result, and the negative result is
    16	most of what we have. Alongside it we report one measurement---the elasticity of
    17	per-step cost with respect to the running count---that we hoped would say in
    18	advance how much room admission control has. It does bound that room on the
    19	configurations we measured, but when we tested whether it explains our
    20	controllers' failure, on their own configuration and on a second workload, it
    21	did not. We report it as a diagnostic of a workload, not as a predictor of
    22	whether a controller will win.
    23	
    24	\subsection{What we measured}
    25	
    26	We serve a replay of 200 real coding-agent sessions on a single RTX 3090
    27	(Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, 96K context, SGLang, a 101{,}432-token KV pool) and compare
    28	a family of admission policies against a sweep of static concurrency caps.
    29	Nine controller variants, spanning four mechanistically distinct ideas, all
    30	land below the static Pareto front, and so does a reimplementation of an
    31	independent congestion-based controller from the literature
    32	(Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier}). The best of them misses by under a point---inside the
    33	$\pm1.0$\,pp band we call a tie, but on the below side of it---and its repeat
    34	misses by 5.6.
    35	
    36	\begin{figure}[tb]
    37	  \centering
    38	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig1_frontier.pdf}
    39	  \caption{Every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO) plane. The grey line
    40	  is the upper-left envelope of the static points---drawn from the data, so it
    41	  uses each configuration's \emph{better} run where a repeat exists, which is
    42	  why \texttt{cap3}+HiCache's 236-minute run sits below the line rather than on
    43	  it; a policy below or on it has
    44	  not beaten a static cap, because time-slicing two caps approximates any
    45	  point on the segment between them. Nine \sys{} variants plus a
    46	  reimplementation of an independent controller, none above at the 10\,s
    47	  latency threshold used throughout; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports how far
    48	  that verdict is conditional on the threshold.}
    49	  \label{fig:frontier}
    50	\end{figure}
    51	
    52	\subsection{Why}
    53	
    54	We decompose the wall clock into \emph{busy} time, during which the engine has
    55	at least one request in flight, and \emph{idle} time, during which it has
    56	none. The busy component is governed by $W_{\text{busy}} \approx
    57	(G/\overline{R})\,\tau$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:busy}), where $\overline{R}$ is the mean
    58	number of running requests, $G$ the generated tokens and $\tau$ the per-step
    59	cost. An admission policy moves $\overline{R}$, and on our configurations it
    60	moves little else---\texttt{bres} is the exception, and \S\ref{sec:model}
    61	uses it to show why moving both components at once is still not enough.
    62	The difficulty is that $\tau$ is not constant in $\overline{R}$: across our
    63	runs $\tau_1$ spans 27.0--36.7\,ms, and on the device-only family, raising the
    64	busy-time running count from 1.00 to 1.84 absorbs a third of what the extra
    65	concurrency buys.
    66	
    67	The reason $\tau$ moves is recomputation, and whether recomputation happens is
    68	decided by the memory tier rather than by the policy. A session's prefix is
    69	worth reusing only while it is still resident, so prefix \emph{reuse} has a
    70	ceiling set by the workload---in our trace, 19.9\,M of the 28.5\,M prompt
    71	tokens, the most any configuration in our data reuses. Whether that ceiling is
    72	realized is set by the tier. With the KV pool on the device alone, reuse
    73	collapses as concurrency rises---69.8\% at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00
    74	down to 27.4\% at 1.84---and the shortfall reappears one for one as
    75	recomputation, 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens. With a host tier in front of it, the
    76	same ceiling holds across the range our controllers occupy---69.8\% to 68.1\%
    77	between 1.25 and 1.73---even though the \emph{device} misses more. Two
    78	configurations at the same running count and the same pool occupancy realize
    79	59.6\% and 68.8\% of the ceiling respectively (\S\ref{sec:model}); what
    80	separates them is not how full the pool is but what happens when it overflows.
    81	That is the finding we would carry to another system first, and it is why this
    82	paper's answer to its own title is a property of the deployment rather than of
    83	the controller.
    84	
    85	The idle component is structural rather than a control failure. A session in a
    86	tool wait issues no request, so when all live sessions happen to be waiting,
    87	the engine is genuinely empty. The stretches are short in the middle and long
    88	in the tail---median 5.6\,s, mean 20.6\,s---and the tail is what a scheduler
    89	feels: 69.6\% of all idle \emph{time} sits in stretches over 20\,s, an order
    90	of magnitude above the 2\,s control cycle. Crucially, the tool wait itself is much longer than
    91	the trace's median suggests: the median inter-round gap is 0.35\,s, but the
    92	\emph{mean} is 16.85\,s with 22\% of gaps over ten seconds. A controller that
    93	reads only the median concludes, as we initially did, that sessions never
    94	leave and therefore that there is no idle time to recover. There is.
    95	
    96	\subsection{Contributions}
    97	
    98	\begin{itemize}
    99	  \item \textbf{We identify the memory tier as what decides whether added
   100	        concurrency is paid for in recomputation, and therefore whether
   101	        admission control has any room.} Prefix reuse has a ceiling set by the
   102	        workload; whether that ceiling is realized is set by the tier.
   103	        Device-only, reuse collapses from 69.8\% to 27.4\% of the prompt
   104	        between $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00 and 1.84 and the shortfall
   105	        reappears as recomputation, 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens; with a host tier
   106	        the ceiling holds, 69.8\% to 68.1\% over 1.25 to 1.73, while the
   107	        \emph{device} misses more (\S\ref{sec:model}). Two configurations at
   108	        the same running count and the same pool occupancy realize 59.6\% and
   109	        68.8\% of the ceiling, so it is not how full the pool is but what
   110	        happens when it overflows. The per-step elasticity follows the same
   111	        line: it passes one above $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$
   112	        device-only and stays below one across the controllers' whole
   113	        operating range with a host tier (\S\ref{sec:criterion}). Two
   114	        measurements that do not share a code path---client-side step timing
   115	        and engine-side token accounting---give the same answer, which is why
   116	        we state the criterion as a property of the workload \emph{and} the
   117	        configuration. This is the finding we would carry to another system
   118	        first, and it is what the title's question turns on.
   119	  \item We test four routes by which an adaptive admission controller was
   120	        expected to beat a tuned static cap on this workload, and we are
   121	        precise about what each one rests on. Two are falsified by measurement
   122	        against criteria we fixed before the runs---changing the accounting
   123	        base (\S\ref{sec:results}) and changing the growth reserve. One is a
   124	        knob rather than a hypothesis, and we report it only because it slides
   125	        the operating point along the front and adds no mechanism. The fourth,
   126	        changing the execution granularity from session count to
   127	        running-request count, is falsified by measurement \emph{and} by an
   128	        argument about why the permit mechanism cannot express the control it
   129	        would need. We do not call the last two of these falsifications of a
   130	        stated hypothesis, because they were not.
   131	  \item We give a two-part decomposition of the wall clock (\S\ref{sec:model})
   132	        that locates the cancellation: admission policy moves the
   133	        running-request count, and the per-step cost moves with it. On the
   134	        device-only configurations we can account for that rise token for
   135	        token---evicted prefixes are recomputed almost one for one. On
   136	        host-tier configurations recomputation is flat and the account has to
   137	        come from elsewhere; we report the part of that residual we were able
   138	        to attribute, and the part we were not (\S\ref{sec:model},
   139	        \S\ref{sec:repro}).
   140	  \item We reduce the question to one measurable number, the elasticity of
   141	        per-step cost with respect to the running count (\S\ref{sec:criterion}),
   142	        and measure it at $0.38$ on the model-consistent step cost and $0.61$
   143	        on the per-request one, on the configurations whose mechanism we can
   144	        account for token for token. We offer it as a diagnostic rather than a
   145	        predictor: it says where on the curve a policy operates and how much
   146	        the remaining headroom is worth, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is explicit
   147	        about the sense in which it is circular and the sense in which it is
   148	        not.
   149	  \item We characterize when adaptivity \emph{could} have paid, and show that
   150	        our workload is not that case: the same throttle configuration that is
   151	        worth $+51\%$ at 25 sessions costs $-42\%$ at 200 (\S\ref{sec:load}).
   152	        Put differently, no single scalar cap is optimal across both, which is
   153	        the regime adaptive control exists for. On the low-load tier, where
   154	        that argument bites hardest, the controller is \emph{not} worse---%
   155	        same-generation measurements put it on the front, $+0.9$\,pp above the
   156	        line and inside the noise. On the high-load tier it is dominated. That
   157	        asymmetry, not a blanket failure, is what this paper measures.
   158	  \item We make the measurements reproducible: the front and each controller's
   159	        signed distance are produced by one script from the raw per-step logs,
   160	        the tables and the sub-thousand-step runs by two others, all named in
   161	        \S\ref{sec:repro}. We report the six claims we had to retract along the
   162	        way, with the checks that caught them.
   163	\end{itemize}
   164	
   165	\subsection{What we do not claim}
   166	
   167	We do not claim that admission control is useless in general. We claim it does
   168	not pay \emph{here}, we show the measurement that decides it, and we argue
   169	(\S\ref{sec:criterion}) that the same measurement can be taken on another
   170	workload before committing to a sweep. We also do not claim our hardware and model are

     1	\section{A Criterion for When Admission Control Can Pay}
     2	\label{sec:criterion}
     3	
     4	The decomposition turns the
     5	question into one number. Throughput during busy time is
     6	$\overline{R}/\tau$, so raising concurrency by a factor $f$ buys a factor
     7	$f^{1-\epsilon}$, where
     8	\begin{equation}
     9	  \epsilon \;=\; \frac{\mathrm{d}\ln \tau}{\mathrm{d}\ln \overline{R}}
    10	  \label{eq:elasticity}
    11	\end{equation}
    12	is the elasticity of per-step cost with respect to the running count. At
    13	$\epsilon = 0$ the step cost is flat, concurrency converts one for one into
    14	throughput, and an admission controller has room to work. At $\epsilon = 1$
    15	the two cancel exactly and it has none. Everything in between is a
    16	quantitative statement about how much room.
    17	
    18	On the five device-only configurations, where \S\ref{sec:model} establishes
    19	that the step cost is driven by recomputation, $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$
    20	runs from 1.00 to 1.84. A least-squares fit of Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity} over
    21	those five points gives $\epsilon = 0.61$ on the per-request measurement
    22	($R^2 = 0.89$), or $0.38$ on the model-consistent one (Fig.~\ref{fig:cancel}).
    23	
    24	That fit is a straight line through a curve, and the curve is what matters. The
    25	segment elasticities are $\epsilon = -0.21$ from \texttt{cap1} to
    26	\texttt{cap2}, $+0.43$ from \texttt{cap2} to \texttt{cap3}, $+1.15$ from
    27	\texttt{cap3} to \texttt{cap4}, and $+1.14$ from \texttt{cap4} to
    28	\texttt{cap5}. Below $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$ added
    29	concurrency still buys something; above it, $\epsilon$ passes one, which means
    30	the engine gets \emph{slower} in wall clock as more requests run. The single
    31	number $0.38$ averages those regimes and describes none of them.
    32	
    33	The operating region is the one that matters, and it is not the region the
    34	controllers run in. All nine controller arms run with HiCache enabled, which
    35	the five device-only caps above do not. Measuring the same elasticity on the
    36	three HiCache configurations we have gives $\epsilon = +0.19$ from
    37	\texttt{cap2}+HC to \texttt{cap3}+HC and $+0.46$ from \texttt{cap3}+HC to
    38	\texttt{cap4}+HC, over $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.25 to 1.73. That range
    39	covers the whole controller operating region (1.35 to 1.69), and over it
    40	$\epsilon$ never approaches one. At matched running counts the HiCache caps pay
    41	$1.7$\,ms less per step at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$.
    42	
    43	This contradicts what an earlier draft of this paper claimed, and it changes
    44	what the criterion is for. The cliff in the device-only curve is a
    45	recomputation effect: \S\ref{sec:model} measures that those runs recompute what
    46	they evict, while under HiCache the host tier absorbs it. The cliff does not
    47	survive on the configuration our controllers actually use. So the criterion
    48	does \emph{not} explain why they lose---on their own configuration they were
    49	not in a region where added concurrency was self-defeating, and the engine had
    50	room they did not take. What the measurement does is rule one explanation out.
    51	The failure is not that this workload denies admission control its lever; it is
    52	that our controllers do not pull the lever they have, which is what
    53	\S\ref{sec:results:idle-memory} and the attribution of
    54	\S\ref{sec:results:attribution} measure directly.
    55	
    56	We are explicit about what this does and does not establish. The HiCache curve
    57	rests on three configurations and two segments, one run each, with no
    58	error bars, and it stops at 1.73---we cannot see whether a cliff appears
    59	beyond the controllers' region. A $\pm10\%$ perturbation of $\tau$ puts the
    60	segment elasticities' bounds across one even on the device-only curve, so the
    61	cliff is a description of the fitted points rather than a threshold we have
    62	located precisely. And it is measured on
    63	different policies rather than by an intervention, so it is a
    64	cross-policy association. On our data it is also computed from the same wall
    65	clock the model then explains---$\tau_1$ is derived from $W_{\text{busy}}$, so
    66	using it to predict $W_{\text{busy}}$ restates rather than tests---and its
    67	points are different policies, so the slope is a cross-policy association, not
    68	a transportable causal derivative. $\tau_2$ is genuinely independent of the
    69	wall clock but measures only decode, so it cannot be dropped into a throughput
    70	equation that includes prefill.
    71	
    72	We tested whether the curve transfers to a second workload and it does not
    73	obviously do so. On a real agent trace (117 sessions, tool waits of seconds
    74	rather than the 95\,ms of the synthetic trace, three static caps and one
    75	controller arm), the model-consistent elasticity is negative across the measured
    76	range---$-2.40$ then $-0.28$, at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.08 to 1.29---and
    77	the controller arm there runs at 1.22, below every one of the nine arms above,
    78	and still does not beat the static front. The per-request measure has the
    79	opposite sign on that trace ($+0.41$, $+0.48$). We report this as a boundary:
    80	the criterion is a description of one workload on one configuration, and where
    81	we could check it elsewhere it neither transferred nor explained the failure.
    82	It is not, as an earlier draft claimed, something that decides the question in
    83	minutes before building anything.
     1	\section{Results: Four Routes, One Outcome}
     2	\label{sec:results}
     3	
     4	\subsection{The frontier}
     5	
     6	Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier} places every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO)
     7	plane and draws the upper-left envelope of the static points. Table~\ref{tab:runs}
     8	gives the numbers and each controller's signed distance from that envelope.
     9	
    10	The result is unambiguous. Of nine controller variants, \emph{none} is above
    11	the envelope---not one, on any run, and the pair we repeated most tightly says
    12	so at a resolution the others do not. The closest arm of all is the one with
    13	single-flight admission disabled, at $0.55$ percentage points \emph{below} the
    14	line---inside our $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, so we record it as \emph{on} the
    15	line rather than below it, but on the below side of it.
    16	
    17	The base configuration is the one whose repeats we can trust, and they are
    18	tight: four runs spanning 0.5\,pp of SLO (89.5\% mean, 279.8 to 293.2 minutes),
    19	against the 3.3\,pp that separates it from the single-flight-off arm. So that
    20	difference is several times the noise of its own configuration rather than
    21	inside it, and it is a trade rather than a win: 28 minutes of wall clock bought
    22	with 3.5\,pp of SLO, moving the arm onto the tie band from below it. Not one
    23	configuration in the table gets above the line, and the tightest pair we have
    24	is what makes us willing to say the near misses are misses. A fifth pair, two runs of \texttt{cap3}+HiCache, \emph{is} used, and an
    25	earlier draft of this paper wrongly set it aside saying one of them exceeded
    26	its own cap. That was a different run: the over-cap one is the device-only
    27	\texttt{cap3} repeat (peak in-flight five against a cap of three, 126
    28	over-cap samples), while both HiCache runs peak at three with none. The
    29	repeat is also both faster and higher on SLO (230.3 minutes, 81.9\% against
    30	236.4 and 81.1\%), so by the same rule that displaced \texttt{cap4}+HiCache's
    31	first run it displaces this one---and moving that anchor is what puts our
    32	best arm below the line rather than on it.\footnote{The repeats are uneven, and
    33	we do not want the tightest pair to stand for all of them. Five configurations
    34	have been run more than once; their SLO spreads are 0.5, 0.8, 1.0, 3.9 and
    35	5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). The two widest belong to the two arms that
    36	decide something---\texttt{horizon}=1 and the leftmost anchor---so where a small
    37	margin matters our repeats are weakest, and where they are strongest (the base
    38	configuration and the \texttt{cap3}+HiCache anchor) the margin is not small. We
    39	report both ends rather than the average.}
    40	
    41	The anchor repeats moved the frontier twice and we report both moves. The
    42	leftmost anchor's second run came out both faster (209.1 against 213.0 minutes)
    43	and higher on SLO (68.5 against 63.4), displacing the first; no controller
    44	verdict moves, because every controller finishes to the right of the next
    45	anchor. The middle anchor's repeat moved it the other way for us: it is also
    46	faster and higher, and taking it moves our best arm from \emph{on} the line to
    47	\emph{on the line, below it}. We report every repeat we ran rather than keeping
    48	the better of each pair, because a frontier whose anchors are single
    49	measurements is the weakness this whole subsection is about---and here it is
    50	the weakness biting.
    51	
    52	The other end of the front deserves its own sentence, because the rest of this
    53	paper is negative and a reader could close it thinking the answer is to do
    54	nothing. The run with \emph{no} admission control---every session admitted the
    55	moment it arrives---finishes in 288.6 minutes at 3.0\% SLO, $87.6$\,pp below
    56	the envelope and the worst SLO we report. Its median
    57	time-to-first-token is 1{,}774 seconds, its prefix cache hit rate collapses from
    58	the planned 75.5\% the runner prints in its end-of-run summary (the all-steps
    59	convention, a different
    60	one from the round-1+ numbers in \S\ref{sec:related}---for the same trace
    61	\texttt{gen\_table.py} reports 73.8\% on that convention) to 1.6\%, and its segment SLO is 30\% in the first
    62	tenth of the run and exactly zero in each of the nine after that. Uncontrolled
    63	admission does not degrade gracefully; it destroys the cache and then serves
    64	almost nobody. The question this paper addresses is therefore not whether to
    65	admit selectively, but which selective rule to use.
    66	
    67	Two features of the front are worth noting before the negative results.
    68	The envelope is steep between 209 and 230 minutes (0.63\,pp per minute) and
    69	shallow thereafter (0.15\,pp per minute), so the cost of buying SLO by
    70	retreating from the fast end is high. And $W$ is not monotone in concurrency:
    71	five sessions (260.9\,min) is \emph{slower} than four (249.9\,min)---the same
    72	configuration family, no host cache on either side---because the per-step cost
    73	rises faster than the request count does. We return to this
    74	non-monotonicity in \S\ref{sec:model}, where it turns out to be the first
    75	appearance of the mechanism that drives the whole frontier.
    76

   133	
   134	\subsection{The controllers leave memory unused}
   135	\label{sec:results:idle-memory}
   136	
   137	Before the routes, one measurement that reframes all of them. Our controller
   138	keeps every session's permit for its lifetime, so its pool occupancy is a
   139	policy output, not a constraint. It runs at a median of 49.2\% of the pool on
   140	the base configuration, and the median number of sessions queueing for
   141	admission is 66. The configuration that finishes fastest---the repeat of that same static cap
   142	of four, at 209.1 minutes---runs at 71.9\%, and its first run at 70.2\%. Occupancy
   143	and running requests track each other reliably across the arms we have (the two
   144	columns in \texttt{occupancy.csv} rank the twenty arms almost identically), but
   145	running count does \emph{not} order wall clock: \texttt{horizon}=2 runs 1.25
   146	requests against the base arm's 1.14 and finishes 15 minutes slower. That gap is
   147	what the next subsection is about.
   148	
   149	The controller believes it is nearly full. Its own decision log records the
   150	projection it admits against---the largest of measured occupancy and the summed
   151	contexts of live sessions, plus a reserve for their growth---at a median of
   152	89{,}073 tokens against a budget of 96{,}360, i.e.\ 7\% free. Its measured
   153	occupancy at the same instants is 49{,}875 tokens: 48.2\% of that budget, or
   154	50.8\% of the pool, is idle. The
   155	projection is 1.79$\times$ the truth on this configuration. Across the
   156	seventeen controller runs we have projection logs for, the ratio runs from 1.24
   157	to 4.11 with a median of 1.59 (\texttt{projection.csv}), so 1.79 is this arm's
   158	value, not a constant of the design; the set is named here because the median
   159	moves with it---over every run in that file it is 1.40. Two terms produce the gap: charging every
   160	live session its full context, when a parked session's KV is evictable and
   161	therefore absent from the engine's count, and the growth reserve, which alone
   162	is 26{,}762 tokens, 28\% of the budget.
   163	
   164	Two things follow, and we are careful about the second.
   165	
   166	The first is that the controllers under-use the pool, and that this is a
   167	property of their estimates rather than of the hardware: the base
   168	configuration holds nearly half the pool idle (48.2\% of its budget, 50.8\% of\
   169	the pool) with 66 sessions queued, because
   170	it believes it has 7\% free when it has 48\%. Any account of why it is slow
   171	that does not mention this is incomplete.
   172	
   173	We then asked what actually stops it, by instrumenting the admission decision.
   174	The path is a conjunction: a session is authorized only if the SLO valve is
   175	open, the round gate is not full, and its projected footprint fits what remains
   176	of the budget. Over the base run's 8{,}196 blocked cycles---cycles with a
   177	session waiting and none admitted---the valve accounts for 6.8\%, the round gate
   178	for none, and a projection already above budget for 38.4\%.
   179	
   180	The remaining 54.8\% are cycles where the controller selected no candidate at

exec
/bin/bash -lc "nl -ba paper/sec_results.tex | sed -n '181,436p'" in /root/paper/foyer
 succeeded in 0ms:
   181	all, and here we are explicit about the limit of what we can resolve. The
   182	controller is single-flight by default: it authorizes at most one session per
   183	two-second cycle and waits for that session to begin a round before authorizing
   184	the next. Whether that gate held or whether a candidate was tried and did not
   185	fit is \emph{not distinguishable from our logs}---the controller does not record
   186	it---so we do not attribute the bucket to either. We bounded it by running that arm: the same configuration with single-flight
   187	disabled. Two things came out of it, and the second is the one that matters.
   188	
   189	The bound first. With the gate off, the residual bucket---which can then only
   190	be \emph{candidates that did not fit}, since the gate cannot be holding---falls
   191	from 54.8\% of blocked cycles to 39.9\%, against 43.5\% that are over budget
   192	outright. So the single-flight path accounts for roughly fifteen points, and
   193	the headroom estimate remains the largest single cause.
   194	
   195	The second thing is that disabling it does not make the controller win.
   196	\texttt{sf=0} finishes in 263.3 minutes at 86.3\% SLO against the base run's
   197	291.3 and 89.8\%: it buys 28 minutes for 3.5\,pp, and its signed distance from
   198	the front moves from $-1.25$ to $-0.57$---onto the tie band from below it, not
   199	above it. Its admission rate is unchanged (2.5\% of cycles admit, against
   200	2.3\%). So the gate was a real cost and removing it moves the arm along the
   201	front rather than past it, which is the same trade every other route in this
   202	section makes.
   203	
   204	Across the eight \sys{} variants that left controller logs---the set is named in
   205	\texttt{gate\_attrib.csv}, and it is eight rather than nine because
   206	\texttt{foyfix} and the two AIMD arms run a different controller and log no
   207	gate state---the projection is over budget in a median 32.2\% of blocked
   208	cycles (19.5\% to 50.8\%) and the valve in a median 32.7\% (6.8\% to 67.9\%).
   209	Both medians move with the set, which is why we name it: over the wider group
   210	of runs in that file they are 37.6\% and 10.5\%. The round gate never binds
   211	except in the two runs that enable it---\texttt{rg2} at 29.3\% and \texttt{rg3}
   212	at 18.0\% of blocked cycles, against 0.6\% or less everywhere else---which is
   213	what one expects of a gate that is off by default.
   214	We also record what this accounting omits, because it changes the picture: the
   215	controller has a starvation guard that bypasses the budget entirely once the
   216	oldest waiter has waited 240\,s, cooling down for 300\,s. On the base run it
   217	accounts for 24.8\% of all admissions (51 of 206), and while the projection is
   218	over budget it is the \emph{only} way in---of 3{,}700 such cycles, nine admitted,
   219	and all nine were forced. It is therefore the guard, not the budget test, that
   220	sets the floor on how fast this controller can admit.
   221	
   222	The second is that under-use does \emph{not} order the outcomes, so it is not
   223	the whole explanation either. \texttt{horizon}=1 and \texttt{cap3}+HiCache sit
   224	at the same median occupancy (57.8\% of the pool) and the same mean running
   225	count (1.52 against 1.51 on the busy-time measure of \S\ref{sec:model}), and
   226	the cap is 19 minutes faster. \texttt{hz0} runs
   227	at 64.1\%, higher than that cap, and is 54 minutes slower. Occupancy buys
   228	running requests reliably---the two columns in \texttt{occupancy.csv} track
   229	each other across all twenty arms---but converting running requests into wall
   230	clock is not simply the per-step cost of \S\ref{sec:model}. Decomposing that
   231	19 minutes into its idle and busy components, 12.6 of them are idle time---%
   232	65\%---and only 6.8 are busy; the two runs' step costs differ by 1.2\,ms
   233	(29.2 against 28.0), about 4\%. A parked session is one the controller has
   234	authorized but not yet released, so the same policy that sets the running count
   235	also sets a large part of the idle time. Both
   236	effects are real; on this workload the unused-memory one is larger for the arms
   237	that finish worst, and the step-cost one is what separates the arms that finish
   238	close together.
   239	
   240	\subsection{What the two mechanisms explain, arm by arm}
   241	\label{sec:results:attribution}
   242	
   243	We have offered two mechanisms, and the honest thing is to say what each one
   244	covers. For every controller arm we took the static cap closest to it in wall
   245	clock and compared their occupancy and their step cost. An arm is attributed to
   246	the unused-memory mechanism if it runs meaningfully fewer requests than its
   247	comparison, and to the step-cost mechanism if it runs a comparable number at a
   248	measurably higher cost. Everything else is a residual.
   249	
   250	\begin{center}
   251	\small
   252	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
   253	\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
   254	  \toprule
   255	  Arm & Occ. & $\overline{R}$ & $\tau_1$ & Attribution \\
   256	  \midrule
   257	  \texttt{horizon}=1  & 57.8 & 1.524 & 29.2 & residual \\
   258	  \sys{} base         & 49.2 & 1.353 & 28.7 & residual \\
   259	  \texttt{horizon}=2  & 54.7 & 1.439 & 33.6 & step cost \\
   260	  \texttt{horizon}=0  & 64.1 & 1.666 & 35.7 & step cost \\
   261	  \texttt{bres}       & 56.9 & 1.481 & 34.1 & step cost \\
   262	  \texttt{breshz1}    & 61.4 & 1.632 &  --- & residual \\
   263	  no gate, no resv.   & 62.1 & 1.678 &  --- & unused mem. \\
   264	  running gate$=$4    & 62.3 & 1.689 &  --- & residual \\
   265	  all throttles off   & 39.2 & 1.675 &  --- & residual \\
   266	  AIMD, retuned       & 66.6 & 1.706 &  --- & residual \\
   267	  AIMD, paper params  & 40.3 & 1.357 &  --- & residual \\
   268	  \bottomrule
   269	\end{tabular}
   270	\end{center}
   271	
   272	Four of the eleven are attributed. Seven are not: their occupancy and their step
   273	cost both sit close to their comparison cap's, and their shortfall is real but
   274	we cannot say from these measurements which mechanism produced it. We publish
   275	the table rather than a summary because a summary would claim more than we
   276	have---and because ``two mechanisms explain the negative result'' would be the
   277	wrong summary. Two mechanisms explain part of it, on four of the eleven arms;
   278	the rest is a measurement we can make and cannot yet interpret.
   279	
   280	\subsection{Route 1: the accounting base}
   281	
   282	Our controller's projection (Eq.~\ref{eq:proj}) charges a session for its full
   283	context even while it is parked in a tool wait, when its KV is evictable and
   284	therefore absent from the engine's reported occupancy. We argued this was a
   285	double charge and removed it, admitting on engine telemetry alone.
   286	
   287	The charge is not double. Removing it did admit more---median engine occupancy
   288	rose from 49.2\% to 56.9\% of the pool---and the round-1+ prefix hit rate held at
   289	80.2\% against the 80.6\% of the run that keeps the charge, so no eviction spiral occurred. But the run was
   290	4.2 minutes \emph{slower} and lost 6.0\,pp of SLO. Per \S\ref{sec:model}, the
   291	extra occupancy raised both $\overline{R}$ (1.35 to 1.48) and $\tau_2$
   292	(23.8\,ms to 31.3\,ms), and the two cancelled.
   293	
   294	We note that this claim had already been falsified once by source inspection:
   295	SGLang's \texttt{token\_usage} subtracts evictable blocks, so a parked
   296	session's prefix is genuinely not counted, and charging for it reserves
   297	capacity that really will be needed back. The end-to-end measurement agrees
   298	with the source reading.
   299	
   300	\subsection{Route 2: the growth reserve}
   301	
   302	Sessions grow by roughly 2.5K tokens per round, so the controller reserves
   303	capacity for the next \texttt{horizon} rounds. We swept the horizon over
   304	$\{0,1,2,3\}$ and the reserve is not a free parameter to be minimised:
   305	Fig.~\ref{fig:horizon} shows the response is non-monotone. Removing the
   306	reserve entirely costs 12.6\,pp of SLO, and a reserve of two rounds is
   307	15 minutes \emph{slower} than three. The optimum is at one round, and there it comes closest to the
   308	static front---0.92\,pp below it, inside the tie band---and that single point is
   309	the one this paper spends the most words on, because it does not reproduce: the
   310	repeat of this configuration lands 5.6\,pp below the line
   311	(\S\ref{sec:results}). Read the sweep as a shape, not as a result at its
   312	minimum.
   313	
   314	\subsection{Route 3: the hard ceiling}
   315	
   316	Lowering the budget fraction is the crudest available knob and behaves as the
   317	other routes do: it slides the operating point along the front. We report it
   318	only for completeness, since it adds no mechanism.
   319	
   320	\subsection{An independent adaptive controller}
   321	\label{sec:results:aimd}
   322	
   323	Every controller above is ours, which invites the objection that the negative
   324	result is a statement about one design rather than about adaptive admission.
   325	We therefore implemented a second, mechanistically different controller: an
   326	AIMD window over an agent-level admission set, following the control law of
   327	the congestion-based system we compare against in \S\ref{sec:related}. The
   328	window grows additively while cache occupancy sits below a low watermark, is
   329	cut multiplicatively when occupancy exceeds a high watermark \emph{and} the
   330	prefix hit rate falls below a threshold, and holds otherwise. Cuts pause the
   331	youngest sessions at their next step boundary; growth resumes paused sessions
   332	first. Every input is a runtime gauge---occupancy and hit rate from the
   333	engine, nothing from the trace---and the window acts through the same named
   334	permits as \sys{}.
   335	
   336	It does not beat the static front either, at either parameter setting
   337	(Table~\ref{tab:runs}). At the values reported in that paper's evaluation it
   338	finishes in 364.3 minutes at 81.4\% SLO, $11.2$\,pp below the envelope; the
   339	same arm with its retuned watermarks finishes in 264.6 minutes at 52.8\%,
   340	$33.4$\,pp below. The retuned setting is also one of the most
   341	volatile runs we report: its segment-to-segment SD is 13.2\,pp of SLO, with
   342	segment values swinging between 37 and 72. Three runs are worse (\texttt{cap4}
   343	at 15.8, the all-throttles-off arm at 14.4, and \texttt{breshz1} at 14.2), and
   344	no other controller arm is as bad---which is what a control loop oscillating
   345	around a capacity cliff looks like.
   346	
   347	We report two things honestly about this comparison. First, this is our
   348	reimplementation of a published control law, not the authors' system, and a
   349	reimplementation of any controller can underperform its paper. We built it to
   350	the published law, gave it the same permit interface and the same 2\,s cycle
   351	as \sys{}, and did not tune it beyond the two documented settings; but the
   352	caveat stands. Second, both controllers stall. At its paper parameters this one pinned its
   353	window at one session, admitted nothing, and left the engine idle for over an
   354	hour. Our own controller does the same thing more briefly: in 4 of the 11
   355	\sys{} runs an audit found a single window of 150--175\,s in which the engine
   356	was empty, the queue held ~90 sessions, the SLO valve was open, and the
   357	controller issued no permit at all---about 0.9\% of each run's wall clock. The
   358	remaining runs, including the best one, show no such window. Nothing is logged
   359	as an error and the run recovers on its own.
   360	
   361	We traced one such window in a repeat of our best configuration and found a
   362	code path that explains it: single-flight admission considered only the head of
   363	the FIFO queue, and when the head was a session too large to fit the remaining
   364	budget it was never admitted, never left the queue, and blocked every smaller
   365	session behind it. That is the code every run in this paper executed. The fix
   366	in our repository---scan the queue, still admit at most one per cycle---is
   367	\emph{not} in the artifact that produced these results, so the numbers here
   368	predate it and we do not claim them for it. We are also explicit that this is
   369	\emph{a} path, not a proven cause:
   370	the audit is a log signature, and the same signature can be produced by other
   371	closed gates. What we can say is that the signature is present in a third of
   372	our \sys{} runs and absent from all of our static caps, which have no
   373	admission decision to get stuck in.
   374	
   375	That is the honest version of a finding we first reported too strongly. An
   376	intermediate draft of this paper called it a deadlock and said the
   377	configuration that produced our best result was the one that deadlocked on its
   378	repeat. Neither holds: the windows are ~160\,s and self-clearing, and the best
   379	configuration never showed one. The windows are also too small (0.9\%) to move
   380	any verdict we report. We keep them because the mechanism is real and its size
   381	is the point: on a pool that holds four median sessions, a controller's failure
   382	mode is a silent stall, not graceful degradation, and it took a repeat run to
   383	see it.
   384	
   385	\subsection{Route 4: execution granularity}
   386	\label{sec:results:gate}
   387	
   388	The first three routes all assume that what a controller can set is \emph{how
   389	many sessions are resident}. A static cap sets the same quantity, so it is
   390	unsurprising that they land in the same place. The fourth route attacks the
   391	assumption instead: because a large fraction of live sessions are parked at
   392	any instant, a cap on \emph{resident} sessions leaves the engine idle whenever
   393	all residents happen to be parked. Capping the \emph{running-request} count
   394	instead should keep the engine busy while leaving the resident count high.
   395	
   396	The permit design supports this directly: a paused session finishes its
   397	current round and blocks before its next one, and resumes when the controller
   398	re-admits it. We implemented it as a gate on the engine's own
   399	\texttt{num\_running\_reqs} and swept the gate over $\{2,4\}$. Only the
   400	gate-of-four arm completed (999 steps); the gate-of-two arm reached 770 and is
   401	not used, so what we report below is a single comparison against the no-gate
   402	arm rather than a sweep.
   403	
   404	It does not work, and the measurement says why. Against the no-gate arm, a
   405	gate of four moved mean running requests from 1.33 to 1.30 and mean live
   406	sessions from 3.25 to 3.03: it tightened both, by 2\% and 7\%, rather than
   407	pinning the one it aims at. That is what we
   408	observe, and we state it as the observation it is rather than deriving a bound
   409	we cannot support. A live session spends most of its time parked in a tool wait
   410	(the ratio of mean running requests to mean live sessions is 0.38 to 0.66
   411	across our arms), so pausing live sessions removes capacity that was not
   412	running in the first place---but that ratio is an average over a run, not a
   413	bound on any instant, and the running count can in principle reach the live
   414	count. Both repairs fail: without a
   415	constraint on admission, a paused session is re-admitted on the next cycle and
   416	blocks for at most one 2\,s cycle; with one, the policy is a session cap again.
   417	The obstacle is granularity---\texttt{paused} takes effect at round
   418	boundaries, while pinning an instantaneous count requires control the
   419	permit mechanism does not have.
   420	
   421	The measured outcome matches: the gate moved running concurrency from 1.33 to
   422	1.30 and live sessions from 3.25 to 3.03. It tightened rather than pinned, and
   423	a tightening is another cap.
   424	
   425	\begin{figure}[tb]
   426	  \centering
   427	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig6_horizon.pdf}
   428	  \caption{The growth reserve is not a free parameter. Removing it costs
   429	  12.6\,pp of SLO; two rounds is 15 minutes slower than three. The optimum at
   430	  one round comes closest to the static front, 0.9\,pp below it---and is the
   431	  one point here that does not reproduce on a repeat (it lands 5.6\,pp
   432	  below).
   433	  The other three settings were each run once, so the shape is better
   434	  supported than any single point on it.}
   435	  \label{fig:horizon}
   436	\end{figure}

     1	\section{Load Dependence}
     2	\label{sec:load}
     3	
     4	A negative result about adaptivity invites the obvious objection: adaptivity
     5	pays when conditions \emph{change}, and a single stationary trace is the one
     6	case where a static cap should be near-optimal. We agree. What we test here is a weaker thing---two
     7	separate constant-load tiers---and \S\ref{sec:discussion} says what that leaves
     8	open.
     9	
    10	We run the same controller and the same cap family on a 25-session tier. The
    11	throttle configuration is reversed: with all safety throttles disabled, the
    12	25-session tier runs \emph{51\% faster} (58.4 to 28.8 minutes) at a cost of
    13	only 2.4\,pp of SLO, while on the 200-session tier disabling the same
    14	throttles costs 42\% (291.3 to 415.0 minutes) and 43.7\,pp of SLO. One switch,
    15	two regimes, opposite signs, both around 40--50\%
    16	(Fig.~\ref{fig:loadtier}). No single scalar setting is optimal across both.
    17	
    18	We flag the generation gap rather than let the figure imply more than we ran.
    19	The two 200-session numbers are current; the two 25-session numbers are from an
    20	earlier harness revision, and we never ran the all-throttles-off arm on the
    21	25-session tier under the current one. So the reversal is a direction we
    22	measured across two harness generations, not a same-generation paired
    23	comparison, and a reviewer should read it that way. The same-generation
    24	25-session measurement we do have is in Table~\ref{tab:tier25}, and it does not
    25	contain that arm.
    26	
    27	This is the regime in which adaptive control should win, and we now have the
    28	same-generation measurement that earlier drafts of this paper were missing.
    29	Four arms on the 25-session tier, all under the current harness
    30	(Table~\ref{tab:tier25}): static caps of two, three and four finish in 32.4,
    31	24.2 and 23.6 minutes at 92.8\%, 91.2\% and 79.2\% SLO, and \sys{} finishes
    32	in 27.6 minutes at 92.8\%.
    33	
    34	\begin{table}[tb]
    35	  \caption{The 25-session tier, all four arms under the current harness.
    36	  This is the same-generation comparison earlier drafts of this paper were
    37	  missing; \texttt{cap4} and the \sys{} arm were run for this revision.}
    38	  \label{tab:tier25}
    39	  \centering
    40	  \small
    41	  \setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
    42	  \begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
    43	    \toprule
    44	    Policy & Wall (min) & Conc. & SLO (\%) & Seg.\ SD (pp) \\
    45	    \midrule
    46	    cap2          & 32.4 & 1.95 & 92.8 & 11.0 \\
    47	    cap3          & 24.2 & 2.77 & 91.2 &  5.1 \\
    48	    cap4          & 23.6 & 3.39 & 79.2 & 21.4 \\
    49	    \sys{}        & 27.6 & 2.47 & 92.8 &  7.1 \\

    53	
    54	The finding is a trade, not a defeat, and it is the opposite of what the
    55	200-session tier shows. Against the same-generation envelope---which runs from
    56	\texttt{cap4} at (23.6, 79.2) through \texttt{cap3} at (24.2, 91.2) to
    57	\texttt{cap2} at (32.4, 92.8)---\sys{} sits $+0.9$\,pp above the line. A
    58	static cap of three is still 12\% faster in wall clock, but it gives up
    59	1.6\,pp of SLO to do it, so neither dominates the other. On this tier the
    60	controller is a different point on the front rather than a dominated one,
    61	which is exactly what the 200-session tier denies it.
    62	
    63	We are careful about the size, and about the yardstick. $+0.9$\,pp is
    64	measured against a straight-line interpolation between static points, not
    65	against a switching policy anyone ran, so the defensible statement is that
    66	\sys{} is not dominated by any static point we measured on this tier---not
    67	that it beats what a static mix could achieve. On 125 requests one request is
    68	worth 0.8\,pp, so the margin is about one request. The tier's
    69	segment-to-segment SD (5.1 to 21.4\,pp) is not the right yardstick: segment
    70	volatility is not run-to-run uncertainty. The right one is the replicate spread
    71	we have---which, across the four configurations we repeated, is 0.5, 1.0, 3.9
    72	and 5.1\,pp. Against the two tightest of those $+0.9$\,pp is about a replicate
    73	wide; against the two widest---which belong to the configurations that decide
    74	our headline elsewhere---it is well inside one. We quote the tightest pair
    75	because it is the least favourable to us, not because it is the whole
    76	distribution. What we will defend is the weaker claim: on the
    77	low-load tier the controller is not worse, and the same switch that is worth
    78	$+51\%$ there costs $-42\%$ at high load. A controller that is competitive
    79	where load is light and dominated where load is heavy is the pattern this
    80	paper set out to explain, and it is the pattern we measured.
    81	
    82	The second finding is the more useful one. The signal an adaptive controller
    83	would need is available---the number of sessions waiting for admission differs
    84	by 6--7$\times$ between the tiers (median 9--11 at 25 sessions against 66 at
    85	200, maximum 135)---but its \emph{interpretation} is not obvious. At the
    86	25-session tier the controller holds eleven sessions back while running
    87	exactly one; that backlog is manufactured by the throttling itself rather than
    88	evidence that demand has slackened. Reading it correctly requires combining
    89	instantaneous backlog with progress through the run, which is a stateful
    90	computation a cap cannot perform. We regard this as the most promising
    91	direction we did not finish, and we are explicit that we did not finish it.

   100	  \label{fig:loadtier}

   102	
   103	\section{Related Work}
   104	\label{sec:related}
   105	
   106	\paragraph{Admission and scheduling for agentic serving.} Recent systems
   107	report large gains from treating an agent session, rather than a request, as
   108	the schedulable unit, and from preserving session state across tool calls:
   109	session-aware admission with CPU-restorable KV
   110	\cite{talaria,continuum}, program-level pause/resume with shortest-context-first
   111	restoration \cite{thunderagent}, and congestion-based control over an
   112	agent-level window \cite{concur}. A recurring measurement in this line is that
   113	recomputation, not admission, dominates: \cite{concur} attributes 49.1\% of
   114	end-to-end latency to recomputation, and \cite{mori} preserves state on the
   115	CPU at the cost of a reload. Our results are consistent with this literature
   116	rather than in tension with it, and \S\ref{sec:discussion} explains why: the
   117	mechanism these systems add is one our controller already has.
   118	
   119	\paragraph{Engine-side techniques.} A separate line improves the engine
   120	rather than the admission decision. Iteration-level batching and paged KV
   121	\cite{yu2022orca,kwon2023pagedattention} are the substrate every policy here runs
   122	on; chunked prefill with stall-free batching \cite{sarathi}, prefill/decode
   123	disaggregation \cite{distserve}, and cache-centric scheduling
   124	\cite{mooncake} are the refinements. Our \S\ref{sec:model} measures 2.6--8.2\,ms of each
   125	27--37\,ms step as work that is not decoding---prefill and non-decode
   126	overhead---which is exactly what this line attacks. We expect these techniques to move the frontier rather than to decide who
   127	sits above it, but we did not run the interaction and so state it as an
   128	expectation.
   129	
   130	\paragraph{State retention and preemption.} A third line keeps session state
   131	alive across the gaps we measure in \S\ref{sec:model}: keeping KV on cheaper
   132	storage across turns \cite{yu2025pensieve,gao2024cachedattention}, migrating it
   133	between instances \cite{sun2024llumnix}, and preempting at iteration granularity
   134	\cite{wu2026fastserve}. These systems exercise finer control than our 2\,s
   135	admission cycle, which is the boundary of our negative result: we falsify one
   136	control granularity, not the family. What they share with \sys{} is that the
   137	state is not discarded---which is why, as we argue below, adding admission
   138	intelligence on top buys nothing here.
   139	
   140	\paragraph{Application-level dependencies and replay.} Serving an LLM
   141	\emph{application} rather than a request means the scheduler sees dependencies
   142	between calls \cite{lin2024parrot}, and agentic traces have structure that
   143	request-level benchmarks do not capture \cite{wang2026xperf}. Our replay
   144	preserves that structure but fixes it in advance, which is exactly the
   145	limitation these works would press on.
   146	
   147	\paragraph{Prefix caching.} The prefix cache is often proposed as the signal
   148	an admission policy should use. In our workload it is not a policy variable:
   149	round-1+ hit rate is a function of the admission policy, and of the host tier,
   150	in a way an earlier draft of this paper got wrong in both directions. Without
   151	HiCache it collapses as the cap rises: 80.8\%, 78.5\%, 70.6\%, 56.0\%, 32.8\%
   152	for caps of one through five, against a planned 80.9\% for the trace. With
   153	HiCache it does not: 80.7\%, 79.6\%, 78.7\% for caps of two, three and four.
   154	Our controller sits at 80.6\%.
   155	So the honest statement is not that admission does not move this quantity---%
   156	\texttt{cap4} alone moves it 25 points---but that the host tier is what keeps
   157	it from moving, which is the same mechanism \S\ref{sec:model} and
   158	\S\ref{sec:criterion} keep running into. A configuration without a host tier
   159	that admits four sessions does not have the cache that a host-tier one at the
   160	same concurrency does. (Both numbers are the
   161	unweighted mean of the per-step planned hit rate over rounds 1 and later; the
   162	token-weighted figure for the same steps is 86.2\%, and the all-steps figure
   163	is 73.8\%. We use the unweighted round-1+ convention throughout, and flag it
   164	because the paper's earlier drafts mixed conventions.) We note the boundary of this claim---we varied admission policy,
   165	not eviction policy, and SGLang exposes alternate eviction policies we did not
   166	test. A characterization of production KV-cache traffic at a large cloud
   167	provider finds that eviction-policy design is ``highly workload-dependent''
   168	and that reuse patterns differ sharply by request category
   169	\cite{wang2025kvcachewild}; that is reason to expect a single trace family to
   170	understate the space, though it is not a policy comparison and we do not
   171	present it as one.
   172	
   173	\section{Discussion}
   174	\label{sec:discussion}
   175	
   176	\paragraph{Why published gains do not reappear.} The systems cited above win
   177	by keeping session state alive across tool waits and by pausing at agent
   178	granularity rather than request granularity. \sys{} has both properties by
   179	construction: the permit file gives per-session pause and resume, and the
   180	controller never frees capacity by discarding a session---it admits fewer
   181	sessions instead. To that extent the mechanism these systems add is one our
   182	controller already has, which is one reason adding admission intelligence on
   183	top of it buys nothing here.
   184	
   185	We are careful not to overstate this, because the distinction is between
   186	\emph{choosing} to discard state and \emph{having} it evicted. Retention here
   187	is the engine's radix cache doing its ordinary job; it is not something the
   188	controller implements, and \S\ref{sec:model} measures that the cache evicts
   189	parked prefixes under pressure and that they are recomputed. What \sys{}
   190	avoids is the deliberate trade of state for capacity. A system that manages the
   191	host tier as an explicit policy---as \cite{mori} and \cite{yu2025pensieve}
   192	do---holds a lever we do not, and our negative result does not cover it.
   193	
   194	\paragraph{The threshold decides the verdict, and we report that.} Every SLO
   195	number in this paper counts a request as good if its time-to-first-token is
   196	under 10\,s. That constant sets the whole vertical axis, so we re-derived the
   197	frontier at 5\,s and 20\,s as well, from the same per-request logs. The
   198	ordering of the static caps does not change. What changes is where the
   199	controllers sit relative to them, and at the loose end one of them crosses:
   200	
   201	\begin{center}
   202	\small
   203	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
   204	\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
   205	  \toprule
   206	  SLO threshold & 5\,s & 10\,s & 20\,s \\
   207	  \midrule
   208	  \texttt{horizon}=1        & $-2.9$ & $-0.9$ & $+1.5$ \\
   209	  \sys{} base               & $-2.3$ & $-1.3$ & $+0.6$ \\
   210	  \texttt{bres}             & $-11.9$ & $-7.8$ & $-4.3$ \\
   211	  Concur-style AIMD        & $-14.2$ & $-11.2$ & $-6.5$ \\
   212	  \bottomrule
   213	\end{tabular}
   214	\end{center}
   215	
   216	Two readings, and we give both. The first is that our headline claim is
   217	conditional: at the strict threshold that interactive serving actually cares
   218	about, no controller reaches the static front; at a loose one, one does. The
   219	second is that the crossing is still not a win, though the reason is more
   220	awkward than we first wrote. $+2.7$\,pp at 20\,s is larger than the replicate
   221	SLO spread of two of the three controller-side configurations we repeated
   222	(1.0 and 0.5\,pp) but smaller than the best arm's own (3.9\,pp) and smaller
   223	than an envelope anchor's (5.1\,pp)---and it is the
   224	anchors, not the controllers, that set the line the margin is measured
   225	against. A third pair
   226	exists---two runs of \texttt{cap3}+HiCache---but one of them exceeded its own
   227	cap, so we do not use it as a replicate. A margin that appears only at one threshold
   228	and only at a size we cannot resolve is not evidence that adaptive admission
   229	works; it is evidence that the question needs more runs than we have. We state
   230	the conditional version in the abstract, and we flag this as the second thing
   231	we would re-run with more GPU time.
   232	
   233	\paragraph{The SLO metric excludes the controller's own cost, and we checked.}
   234	Every SLO number above counts a request as good if its time-to-first-token is
   235	under 10\,s, and the runner measures that from the moment the request is
   236	\emph{submitted to the engine}. A session that waits for a permit has not been
   237	submitted yet, so the wait is invisible---and that wait is precisely the cost
   238	an admission controller imposes. We therefore recomputed the runs we have per-step
   239	logs for on a ready-to-first-token basis, where a round's clock starts when the
   240	session is actually able to run (its arrival for round~0, the previous round's
   241	completion plus its tool wait otherwise). That is nineteen of the twenty-three
   242	200-session runs in Table~\ref{tab:runs}; the no-control baseline and the two runs whose
   243	per-step logs we did not recompute are absent, and we do not extend the claim
   244	to them.
   245	
   246	The correction is large. Every configuration loses 3--19\,pp of SLO, and the
   247	loss is systematic: tight caps queue the hardest, so \texttt{cap1} falls from
   248	94.8\% to 76.2\% while \texttt{cap5} falls only from 27.1\% to 24.2\%. A
   249	session admitted last under \texttt{cap1} can wait hours for its turn; its
   250	engine-side TTFT is still 1.6\,s.
   251	
   252	What does \emph{not} change is the verdict, within the subset we recomputed.
   253	The static envelope keeps the same four anchors, and of the eleven controller
   254	arms, nine move by no more than
   255	2.7\,pp and none crosses the $\pm1.0$\,pp band into a new verdict. Two move
   256	more: the all-throttles-off arm improves by 8.3\,pp and the retuned AIMD arm by
   257	2.8\,pp, both of which stay below the front either way. The one that decides our headline moves the wrong way for
   258	the controller: on the ready clock \texttt{horizon}=1's own SLO falls from
   259	84.8\% to 68.2\% while the anchor it is measured against falls less, so the
   260	ready clock moves it further below the line rather than closer to it. We do not
   261	give a ready-clock distance for it, because the two anchor repeats that define
   262	the line are not in the set we could recompute (\S\ref{sec:discussion} says
   263	which runs are), and inventing a line for them would be the same error this
   264	subsection is about.
   265	
   266	The subset matters and we state its edge. The two configurations whose repeats
   267	decide how we read the verdict---\texttt{horizon}=1's repeat and the fastest
   268	anchor \texttt{cap4}+HC's---are not in the nineteen, because we did not
   269	recompute their per-step logs on the ready clock; the rebuilt envelope
   270	therefore rests on the original anchors without their repeats, and a
   271	ready-clock frontier that included them could move the line by the 3.9 and
   272	5.1\,pp those repeats differ by. We do not claim the verdict is unchanged under
   273	every SLO definition---only that on the runs we could recompute, moving the
   274	clock to the ready point costs every configuration 3--19\,pp and changes no
   275	verdict in that set.
   276	We report this because a reviewer should ask it,
   277	and because the answer is the useful part: the negative result is not an
   278	artifact of choosing an SLO definition that flatters controllers. If anything
   279	the flattering choice was the one that moved our best arm's distance from
   280	$-0.92$ to $+0.22$---that is, from just below the line to just above it, with
   281	neither number outside the tie band.
   282	
   283	\paragraph{Limitations.} Our results come from one 24\,GB accelerator, one 7B
   284	model and one trace family; generality is untested. Only four configurations
   285	have been repeated---twice, twice, twice and three times---so the replicate
   286	spread we report in \S\ref{sec:results} is estimated from one to two degrees of
   287	freedom per configuration; a margin as small as the one that decides our verdict would need several
   288	more repeats per configuration to resolve, and we did not run them. Two specific boundaries
   289	matter. First, the pool holds about four median sessions, so concurrency is
   290	capacity-bound by construction; on a device with a much larger pool the
   291	premise may not hold and the conclusion may invert---we regard reproducing on
   292	a larger device as the single most important next experiment. Second, the
   293	load is stationary, single-tenant and single-SLO. The load-dependence result
   294	of \S\ref{sec:load} is measured on two \emph{separate} constant-load tiers, so
   295	it says what to expect when conditions differ, not what happens while they
   296	change; a trace whose load moves during the run is the experiment that would
   297	settle it, and we did not run one. Multi-tenant and heterogeneous-SLO settings
   298	are where we would look next.
   299	
   300	\paragraph{Reproducibility.}
   301	\label{sec:repro}
   302	The front and the signed distance of each controller are computed by
   303	\texttt{scripts/lab/frontier.py} from the raw per-step logs; the run tables and
   304	the four runs shorter than 999 steps are computed by
   305	\texttt{scripts/lab/gen\_table.py} and \texttt{scripts/lab/run\_summary.py}
   306	respectively, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is computed by
   307	\texttt{gen\_elasticity.py} and \texttt{gen\_elasticity\_hc.py}. Every run is
   308	guarded against a shrunken KV pool.
   309	
   310	\emph{Co-tenancy on the measurement host.} Our runs share a host with other
   311	tenants and with each other, and late in the campaign we found that this
   312	matters more than the pool guard catches. An audit
   313	(\texttt{gen\_concurrency.py}, data in \texttt{paper/data/concurrency.csv})
   314	reconstructs, for every run, how many other runs were alive for how long. Runs
   315	that overlapped three or more others for at least an hour have a per-step cost
   316	inflated by 25--40\%: the per-request decode interval $\tau_2$, which is
   317	measured client-side and never touches the sampler, sits at 30.9--33.3\,ms for
   318	those runs against 23.8--25.7\,ms for the same arms measured alone. Two
   319	controls pin the cause to co-tenancy rather than to the configuration. One
   320	run (\texttt{cap1}) holds $\tau_2$ at 20.3--20.7\,ms for its first five and a
   321	half hours and jumps to 22.4--27.5\,ms in the minute two other runs start. And
   322	two independent runs (\texttt{F:t85}, \texttt{F:rl99}) step down from
   323	$\approx$30\,ms to 22--25\,ms in the same wall-clock minute, when a third run
   324	ends. Twelve of our runs fall in the affected class. Eight are arms we do not
   325	report here; four are controller variants in Table~\ref{tab:runs}
   326	(\texttt{F:bres}, \texttt{F:hz0}, \texttt{F:hz2}, \texttt{F:breshz1}), and
   327	those four have been re-measured under the same one-run-per-GPU discipline as
   328	everything else. We report the episode because the failure is silent in a way
   329	worth knowing about: the engine-side counters---running count, pool occupancy,
   330	evictions, recomputation---are indistinguishable between the affected runs and
   331	the clean ones. The contaminated runs' \emph{volume} measurements are
   332	unaffected: the four affected host-tier arms reuse 0.691--0.694 of the prompt,
   333	inside the 0.681--0.698 range of the clean host-tier arms, so only their timing
   334	columns are unusable.
   335	
   336	One caveat about the controller artifact, which a reader with our code would
   337	otherwise hit. There is no single controller copy behind this paper: the runs
   338	record four different hashes of \texttt{controller\_budget3.py}
   339	(\texttt{2b573829083c} on 31 runs, \texttt{f825bc768188} on 19,
   340	\texttt{d692ab11f396} on 4 and \texttt{e1dd5ae78019} on 3), so the artifact
   341	evolved during the campaign and a reader cannot reproduce every number from
   342	one revision. None of the four matches the repository copy
   343	(\texttt{a21bd2b63774}), and the difference is not one hunk but several: the
   344	queue-scan fix described in \S\ref{sec:results:gate}, and a rename of the
   345	logging variable whose rebinding \S\ref{sec:results} identifies as the source
   346	of a retraction. The repository has both; none of the four running copies has
   347	either.
   348	The numbers in this paper therefore describe the pre-fix path, with one
   349	partial exception we want to state precisely rather than let a reader assume
   350	the stronger thing. We did run the same configuration with \texttt{sf=0}
   351	(\S\ref{sec:results:idle-memory}), and on the running copy that changes two
   352	things at once: it scans the queue \emph{and} drops the one-per-cycle limit,
   353	because the \texttt{break} that enforces single-flight is inside the same
   354	conditional. The repository's fix keeps the limit and only adds the scan. So our
   355	run is not a test of the fix as written---it is a test of the fix plus a looser
   356	admission rate, and it moves the arm along the front rather than past it. The
   357	narrower experiment, scan-with-the-limit-kept, is the one we would do first with
   358	more GPU time. We report six claims we retracted during
   359	the work, because the retractions
   360	were the most informative events. In order of how much they cost us:
   361	
   362	\emph{The denominator of the step cost.} Our first \S\ref{sec:model} divided by
   363	the whole-run mean running count while multiplying by busy time. The two are
   364	not the same quantity---the whole-run mean includes the idle samples---so
   365	$\tau$ came out low by exactly the busy fraction, 36\% for \texttt{cap1}, and
   366	two independent errors cancelled to make our two measurement methods appear to
   367	agree. This is the one that changed a headline number.
   368	
   369	\emph{A mechanism refutation that was itself refuted}: we withdrew the
   370	eviction-to-recomputation account on the strength of two checks that were both
   371	identities rather than measurements (\S\ref{sec:model}).
   372	
   373	\emph{A median-based reading of the tool wait} that inverted a central
   374	assumption. \emph{A monotone relation between idle fraction and concurrency}
   375	that the full data does not show. \emph{A growth-sensitivity conclusion} that
   376	rested on an arm we had already invalidated. And \emph{the accounting
   377	double-charge} of \S\ref{sec:results}, falsified twice---once by source
   378	inspection and once by the end-to-end measurement here.
   379	
   380	\section{Conclusion}
   381	
   382	We asked whether an adaptive admission controller can beat a tuned static
   383	concurrency cap on a memory-bound agentic LLM workload, and found that on our
   384	system it cannot: nine variants, four routes tested, none above the static
   385	front. We expected the reason to be a cancellation---admission policy controls
   386	how many requests are in flight, the per-step cost rises with that number, and
   387	the product is what the wall clock sees---and we built a two-part decomposition
   388	of the wall clock that makes the cancellation measurable. That decomposition
   389	holds, and the cost it measures is real, but it is not the whole reason and on
   390	the configuration our controllers run it is not the binding one: there the
   391	elasticity stays below one across the operating range, so added concurrency was
   392	still cheap and the engine had room. Our controllers did not take it. What we
   393	can say at the end is narrower than what we set out to say: on this hardware and
   394	this workload, the admission rules we could build and tune did not beat a static
   395	cap, the capacity estimate they act on is 1.79$\times$ the truth, and a
   396	structural idle component that no policy we built recovers at a profit remains.
   397	The regime in which adaptivity should have paid---where the same switch is worth
   398	$+51\%$ and $-42\%$---is one where the controller is not dominated by any static
   399	point we measured, and does not dominate one either. The measurement we offer is
   400	a diagnostic an engineer can take before committing to a sweep; whether it
   401	predicts the outcome on another workload is a question we tested once and could
   402	not confirm.
